Table 29 also retains LayerNorm’s +1.48-point efect and count-mean propagation’s +0.26.

Other hyperedge-prediction cases. The remaining hyperedge-prediction cases provide complementary evidence. On Actor, the selected TFHNN configuration uses node features without incidence-based message passing, so its unchanged 10.15-point advantage after the intervention does not require information from the hypergraph structure. For Cora-CA and Pokec, we also evaluate separately constructed pairwise lower-order controls that retain pair multiplicities and average pairwise messages. The Cora-CA control matches the fully perturbed score of UniGCNII, and the Pokec control matches the fully perturbed score of HGNN. Together, these cases show that several retained gaps can be matched by pairwise or feature-only mechanisms.

Additional node-classification analyses. Four further cases suggest that pairwise processing and model design can help explain retained advantages, although the controls provide diferent levels of evidence. On Cora-CA, matched comparisons attribute an 8.49-point accuracy gain to pairwise weighting and a further 2.96 points to architecture and difusion choices. Adding TFHNN’s native non-pairwise component instead changes accuracy by −0.45 points. Thus, in these comparisons, the gains come from lower-order processing rather than the native grouping component. On DBLP-CA, disabling PhenomNN’s propagation reduces accuracy by about 5.1 points. Pairwise operators using count-mean or pair-diagonal normalization also outperform binary clique expansion. These results indicate that both propagation and the choice of pairwise normalization matter. On Cora, removing difusion from the fully pairwise TFHNN control reduces accuracy by 4.51 points, close to the selected model’s 4.52-point remaining advantage. On ModelNet40, restricting EDHNN to feature-only processing reduces accuracy by about 1.9 points, also close to its remaining advantage; separate comparisons identify pairwise weighting and normalization as the largest source of improvement among the tested changes. These ablations show that propagation helps the tested models. However, the similarity between the performance losses and the remaining gaps does not establish that propagation alone explains those gaps.

Pairwise controls for hypergraph classification. On RHG-10, the Pair-Multiplicity control outperforms CEGAT by 2.25 points, exceeding HNHN’s 1.72-point advantage at the fully perturbed endpoint. On RHG-3, Pair-SAGE-Mean outperforms CEGCN by 1.03 points, recovering about 78% of HNHN’s 1.33-point remaining advantage. Both controls use only weighted clique-expansion information. They therefore demonstrate that lower-order models can recover all or much of these advantages. These additional controls were outside the original model-selection pool and do not replace the selected model pairs reported in the main results.

Pubmed and Ratings: feature processing versus propagation. To examine what sustains LEGCN’s advantage, we disable messages between distinct node–hyperedge incidences (a node’s memberships in hyperedges) while retaining its layers, hidden dimensions, nonlinearities, training settings, and feature paths. This control tests how much of the advantage remains without information being passed between incidences. The resulting self-only model outperforms the selected MLP baseline by 1.29 accuracy points on Pubmed, while restoring propagation adds only 0.09 points. On Ratings, the corresponding gains are 1.22 and 0.21 points. Thus, most of LEGCN’s advantage in these comparisons remains without passing information between incidences, suggesting that diferences in feature processing and model configuration account for much of the gap.

What the 17 HNNs compute at the fully perturbed endpoint. The complete input at the fully perturbed endpoint contains two-node hyperedge instances and retained singleton instances. All endpoint incidences are determined by weighted pairwise connections and singleton multiplicities. This removes the original grouping into larger hyperedges, but does not make every model an ordinary GCN. Models can still difer in normalization, nonlinear messages, representations of nodes and edges, paths that reintroduce the initial features (restart), and how representations are converted into predictions (readout). Table 30 summarizes what the 17 HNNs do at this endpoint.

For the following operator derivations, let $s _ { v }$ denote the number of retained singleton hyperedge instances at node $v ,$ and let s collect these counts. This notation describes the fixed background incidences retained by the implementation. Let C denote of-diagonal pair multiplicities and $d = C \mathbf { 1 }$ . Before any additional model-specific self loops, write

$$
\begin{array} { r } { K = \frac 1 2 \big ( C + \mathrm { d i a g } ( d ) \big ) + \mathrm { d i a g } ( s ) , \qquad q = d + s , \qquad D _ { q } = \mathrm { d i a g } ( q ) . } \end{array}
$$

On rows with $q _ { v } > 0$ , mean node–edge–node aggregation becomes

$$
Q _ { \mathrm { m e a n } } = D _ { q } ^ { - 1 } K , \qquad Q _ { \mathrm { s y m } } = D _ { q } ^ { - 1 / 2 } K D _ { q } ^ { - 1 / 2 } .\tag{9}
$$

Inverse degrees are set to zero where $q _ { v } = 0$ before applying any explicit cold-node policy. When $s = 0$ , these expressions reduce to $\begin{array} { r } { Q _ { \mathrm { m e a n } } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } I + \frac { 1 } { 2 } D _ { d } ^ { - 1 } C } \end{array}$ on nodes with $d _ { v } > 0$ . Thus even unweighted pair instances induce a structural self contribution when messages travel to a pair and back. Singleton instances contribute only self messages and also enter the incidence-degree normalization.

HGNN uses the row-normalized form and HCHA the symmetric form; both instantiate the same convolution class without enabling its attention branch. The layer still retains its learned feature map, bias, and inter-layer activation.

UniGCNII’s mean aggregation has the same $Q _ { \mathrm { m e a n } }$ step, followed by its configured normalization, restart, and learned residual map. TFHNN instead difuses inputs using $\mathcal { N } ( K + I )$ , with its configured restart and depth, before applying its decoder. PhenomNN uses both $\mathcal { N } ( C + \mathrm { d i a g } ( q ) + I )$ and $\mathcal { N } ( K + I )$ inside its iterative update. These distinctions explain why preserving pair counts and singleton multiplicities does not make the architectures or their performance interchangeable.

## F.2 O/D/L additions shared across datasets and primary-metric results

The full study compares oficial CEGCN, +O, +D, +L, and +O+D+L on all 13 node-classification and six hypergraph-classification datasets, and under both identity and zero-message policies on all six hyperedgeprediction datasets. The same difusion depths, mixing weights, and normalization settings are used across datasets. They were motivated by the observed case-study results and evaluated without further hyperparameter tuning.

O: multiplicities and structural self weights. Let B denote binary of-diagonal adjacency, $C$ the ofdiagonal pair multiplicities, and $d = C \mathbf { 1 }$ . Here s records retained singleton multiplicities as fixed background incidences. We separate the operator change into $\mathbf { O } _ { 1 }$ , replacing B by $C ,$ and $\mathbf { O } _ { 2 } ,$ replacing unit self weights by the structural diagonal, including retained singleton contributions, after pair multiplicities have been added by $\mathrm { O _ { 1 } }$ :

$$
\begin{array} { r } { P _ { \mathrm { b i n a r y } } = \mathcal { N } ( B + I ) , \qquad P _ { \mathrm { O _ { 1 } } } = \mathcal { N } ( C + I ) , \qquad P _ { \mathrm { O _ { 1 } } + \mathrm { O _ { 2 } } } = \mathcal { N } ( C + \mathrm { d i a g } ( d + 2 s + 2 \mathbf { 1 } ) ) . } \end{array}
$$

Here $\mathcal { N } ( A ) = D _ { A } ^ { - 1 / 2 } A D _ { A } ^ { - 1 / 2 }$ , with $D _ { A } = \mathrm { d i a g } ( A { \bf 1 } )$ . The existing label O denotes the combined $\mathrm { O } _ { 1 } { + } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ operator. The term 2s accounts for singleton incidences in the implemented operator and vanishes when no singleton hyperedges are present. Changes along this ladder depend on their order: $\mathrm { { O _ { 2 } } }$ changes the normalization of neighbor messages as well as self messages.

Matched decomposition of O across benchmarks. We compare binary CEGCN, $+ \mathrm { O _ { 1 } } .$ and $+ \mathrm { O } _ { 1 } { + } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ on all 25 benchmarks, using both identity and zero-message policies for hyperedge prediction. D and L are disabled. Learned-layer architectures, activations, prediction heads, splits, and training settings are matched within each comparison; each operator variant is trained separately. For each seed, the $\mathrm { O _ { 1 } }$ increment is the counts-only score minus binary CEGCN, and the $\mathrm { O _ { 2 } }$ increment is the full-O score minus counts-only CEGCN. We report original-view scores and the means and sample standard deviations of these paired changes.

D: residual difusion. Let B be the exact normalized binary clique-expansion operator used by oficial CEGCN. Before any trainable transformation, combine the original features with features propagated for diferent numbers of steps:

$$
\widetilde { X } = 0 . 8 X + \frac { 0 . 2 } { 6 } \sum _ { \substack { k \in \{ 1 , 2 , 4 , 8 , 1 6 , 3 2 \} } } Q ^ { k } X , \qquad Q = \left\{ { B } , \quad \mathrm { C E G C N + D } , \quad \right.
$$

L: classifier normalization. L inserts LayerNorm after the hidden activation and before dropout, with $\epsilon = 1 0 ^ { - 5 }$ and learned afine scale and bias. O and D add no trainable parameters.

Hyperedge-prediction policies. Cold nodes do not occur in any positive training hyperedge. The identity policy retains their transformed features; the zero-message policy removes their feature-dependent messages at each learned layer.

Full primary-metric results. Tables 31 to 42 report absolute scores and paired changes for every benchmark under the five main recipes. For hyperedge prediction, changes are measured against CEGCN using the same policy for cold nodes; Table 43 isolates identity versus zero-message policies for cold nodes within each recipe.

Stronger-baseline comparisons using secondary metrics. Tables 44 to 53 report secondary-metric results.
